Federal laws, for example, are passed by Congress and signed by the President. The judicial branch, in turn, has the authority to decide the constitutionality of federal laws and resolve other cases involving federal laws. JUD-SIG-000255JUD-LBL-000611JUDSNAP-0024ef2b70b816cc113d486ad5e24568dfc06c36d29ec80210b36784e08bfc245d5COURT_OFFICE_SIGNALLike senior status Article III judges, bankruptcy and magistrate judges may continue to provide judicial assistance after they have retired. Generally, recalled judges exercise all the powers and duties that they had as an active judge. Circuit judicial councils determine whether there is a need for recall services from bankruptcy and magistrate judges based on the court's workload. In addition, recall requests that seek staffing or that cost more than a certain amount in additional salary and travel expenses must be approved by a Judicial Conference committee. JUD-SIG-000389JUD-LBL-000881JUDSNAP-003bac6ed503dc846886ed80c7178f48966de5a14f1fa1e0fd950fd1eca6cfa7d66APPEAL_FINALITY_SIGNALUnited States district courts and courts of appeals often prescribe local rules governing practice and procedure. Such rules must be consistent with both Acts of Congress and the Federal Rules of Practice and Procedure, and may only be prescribed after notice and an opportunity for public comment. A court's authority to prescribe local rules is governed by both statute and the Federal Rules of Practice and Procedure. See 28 U.S.C. §§ 2071(a)-(b); Fed. R. App. P. 47; Fed. R. Bankr. Such an order will, for example, deny a petition for certiorari without comment. Regularly scheduled lists of orders are issued on each Monday that the Court sits, but "miscellaneous" orders may be issued in individual cases at any time. Scheduled order lists are posted on this Website on the day of their issuance, while miscellaneous orders are posted on the day of issuance or the next day.024716725a2ff74f10f77ed3af0d1995a0c0f84afb00523dafe468c4573c20e6PROPOSED_NOT_ADMITTED

JUD-SIG-000637JUD-LBL-001438JUDSNAP-007595f20aa6ddbf605720c09cfa1fbff4ed5b131275d5df50f49e248a9b359904cCASE_DOCKET_SIGNALAs a second-year associate with Minnesota law firm Junell, Driscoll, Fletcher, Dorsey & Barker, future Justice Harry A. Blackmun first contributed to a Supreme Court case in 1935. Initially placed in the firm’s tax department, one of his early assignments was to write a Supreme Court brief in an alimony and trust tax law case. On behalf of the petitioner in Douglas v. Willcuts (1935), this brief became part of the first case argued in the newly finished Supreme Court Building on October 14, 1935. Blackmun continued to work for the firm for 16 years before joining one of the firm’s clients, the Mayo Clinic, as their first resident counsel in 1950. President Richard Nixon appointed Blackmun as an Associate Justice in 1970 and he served on the Supreme Court until 1994.92b061954c4431807f4447c4daffebb90a0b08caaf589a117c83be79edfa489cPROPOSED_NOT_ADMITTED

JUD-SIG-000762JUD-LBL-001768JUDSNAP-00852e33c8c7d402214406f13d41622b1480f725f14ff875993e0b5f3777b14292eCONDUCT_DISABILITY_SIGNALThe Digest of Authorities on the Judicial Conduct and Disability Act (Digest) is a summary of publicly available materials on the Judicial Conduct & Disability Act. The Digest summarizes the relevant sources of law, Rules on Judicial Conduct and Judicial Disability Proceedings (Rules), and orders on complaints of judicial misconduct issued by chief circuit judges, judicial councils, and the Committee on Judicial Conduct and Disability. The Digest has been developed in line with a recommendation from the 2006 Report on the Implementation of the Judicial Conduct and Disability Act of 1980 (Breyer Committee Report), which recommended that the Committee on Judicial Conduct and Disability develop an online resource for all chief circuit judges, judicial council members, and circuit staff to help them “understand the Act and how to apply it.”93e8a6a2c923ce29224ecf1e3147b4daf6e0dc67b4e5039a469b212efa1055a3PROPOSED_NOT_ADMITTED

JUD-SIG-000785JUD-LBL-001963JUDSNAP-009351c83f644b83a5c32d3ede22aa53b16554c22a84eaa1ac9f83fab8ca67c2054IMPEACHMENT_REMOVAL_SIGNALThe Constitution gives the House of Representatives “the sole Power of Impeachment” (Article I, Section 2) of federal officers and gives the Senate “the sole Power to try all Impeachments” (Article I, Section 3). In the constitutional procedure of impeachment and removal, the House serves in the role of a grand jury bringing charges against an officer suspected of “Treason, Bribery, or other high Crimes and Misdemeanors” (Article II, Section 4).f0827b0d930bb1942d3a127015f7373d3baa312aac170120020147a06f42e6a5PROPOSED_NOT_ADMITTED

From the early 20th century forward, the preferred method of selecting managers has been by a House Resolution naming the number and the persons of the committee of managers. In some instances, the House has, by resolution, fixed the number of managers and authorized the Speaker to appoint them.
